\title{A Textbook about the Paper: "Group Sequence Policy Optimization"}

# Chapter 1: Introduction to Reinforcement Learning in Large Language Models

## The Evolution of LLM Training Paradigms: From Pre-training to RLHF/RLAIF

The journey toward developing intelligent language models has been characterized by a steady expansion of objectives, moving from simple pattern matching to the nuanced deployment of reasoning capabilities. Initially, the training pipeline was dominated by pre-training on vast corpora of text, allowing models to learn statistical regularities and general world knowledge. However, as researchers sought to align these powerful generative systems with human intent, values, and safety guidelines, the industry transitioned toward post-pretraining refinement methods known collectively as RLHF (Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback) and RLAIF (Reinforcement Learning from AI Feedback). Traditionally, this stage relied heavily on Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO), an algorithm designed to constrain policy updates within a safe vicinity of a reference distribution. As described in the literature surrounding these advancements, PPO operates by estimating advantages via a separate value model—a neural network approximating expected future returns—and then optimizing the policy probability mass through a surrogate objective involving importance ratios calculated at every single token step. While effective for smaller scales or simpler alignment tasks, this architecture introduced significant complexity; the necessity of maintaining a value model nearly as large as the policy itself created substantial memory burdens and computational costs. More critically, the fidelity of PPO became entirely dependent on the accuracy of this auxiliary value estimator. If the value model failed to accurately predict return distributions—particularly as task difficulty escalated—the entire optimization process would become unstable or biased. Consequently, while PPO laid the groundwork for aligned models, its structural dependencies limited its scalability when pushing beyond elementary instruction-following behaviors into domains requiring deep logical deduction or extended planning chains.

As research progressed into the era of frontier reasoning models, practitioners identified that relying solely on static human preference datasets was insufficient for unlocking true cognitive depth. This realization catalyzed a shift away from pure supervised fine-tuning and early forms of RL toward dynamic feedback loops where models could generate diverse outputs evaluated verifiably against ground truth solutions in mathematics or code execution environments. This led to variants such as Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), which attempted to eliminate the expensive value model bottleneck by calculating relative advantages directly from groups of responses generated for a single prompt. By comparing scores assigned by automated verifiers across multiple generations ($G$ responses per query), GRPO computed advantages based on mean-centered differences in verification rewards rather than predicted state values. Despite removing the explicit value head requirement—which simplified architectural demands somewhat—the underlying mechanics of GRPO retained the fundamental flaw inherited from earlier token-centric approaches: it performed importance weighting and clipping operations individually for every token position $t$ within a response $y$. While computationally lighter than traditional PPO in terms of parameters required during training steps, this granular approach obscured a deeper probabilistic inconsistency inherent in scaling up system sizes and output lengths. The evolution thus moved from generic supervision to direct outcome-based reinforcement learning but remained trapped in an implementation detail where local token decisions were treated independently despite global rewards being contingent upon complete sequence correctness. Understanding this lineage reveals why simply adding more compute or expanding dataset diversity yielded diminishing returns if the optimization machinery itself suffered from intrinsic instabilities rooted in how probabilities were aggregated across variable-length trajectories.

## Scaling Laws in RL: Why Massive Computation is Necessary for Deep Reasoning

The pursuit of deep reasoning skills in large language models represents a qualitative leap from generating fluent text to solving novel problems such as competition-level mathematics or writing bug-free software architectures that satisfy rigorous specifications. Achieving these capabilities requires scaling reinforcement learning not merely linearly but exponentially with respect to computational resources allocated per training iteration and total environment interactions available during exploration phases unlike prior regimes where incremental gains sufficed now breakthrough performances emerge only after deploying hundreds or thousands times discovered唯有通过持续高强度的强化学习反馈循环才能激发潜能释放突破传统监督微调边界界限实现真正的智能跃迁。简而言之，为了训练能够处理复杂推理任务的模型，必须投入巨大的计算资源来生成大量的高质量样本（rollout），并经过多轮迭代优化策略网络。这种“大规模”不仅仅是指参数数量的增加，更体现在每一步更新中采样规模的扩大、批处理的复杂度提升以及整体训练周期的延长上。只有在这种高吞吐量的实验环境下，算法才能真正收敛到最优解区域，避免陷入局部极小值或导致灾难性的模式崩塌。此外，随着响应长度的增长和任务难度的上升，所需的探索空间呈指数级扩张，这意味着每一次梯度估计都必须基于足够多的独立样本来保证统计显著性；否则微小的噪声积累将迅速放大为系统级的不稳定性最终摧毁已习得的知识结构使得整个训练过程前功尽弃因此现代前沿模型的训练本质上是一场关于算力规模与数据质量之间精密平衡的艺术追求而非单纯的技术堆砌结果。正是通过对这一scaling # Chapter 2: Foundations of Policy Optimization Algorithms

## Introduction to Reinforcement Learning Scaling

As you delve into the cutting-edge applications of deep reinforcement learning (RL) for Large Language Models (LLMs), understanding the foundational algorithms is critical before exploring novel methodologies. Modern LLM development increasingly relies on RL paradigms to unlock capabilities beyond supervised pre-training, enabling models to solve complex mathematical proofs, generate intricate code, and engage in multi-step logical reasoning. However, translating classical RL success stories—originally designed for discrete Markov Decision Processes—to the realm of massive autoregressive generative models requires careful adaptation of existing techniques. The transition involves reconciling the infinite action spaces inherent in text generation with finite horizon episodes and managing the statistical properties of vast datasets efficiently.

Two primary pillars support the current landscape of RLHF (Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback) and subsequent direct preference optimization variants: Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) and its recent successor, Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). Both methods attempt to navigate the treacherous waters of "off-policy" learning, where data collected from an older version of the model must be used to train a newer version without causing destructive divergence or catastrophic forgetting. This chapter aims to deconstruct these foundational approaches, providing you with a rigorous grasp of their mathematical structures, intuitive motivations, and operational mechanics. By thoroughly analyzing how these algorithms handle probability distributions, advantage estimation, and constraint enforcement, we lay the necessary groundwork for appreciating why certain instabilities arise in extreme scales and what principles drive the evolution toward sequence-level optimizations.

Your journey begins with establishing a precise formalism for autoregressive generation. Without a shared vocabulary regarding policies, probabilities, and rewards, distinguishing between subtle variations in algorithmic design would be impossible. Consequently, we start by defining the core components of the environment—the query space—and how an autoregressive model acts as a stochastic policy mapping inputs to outputs. From there, we trace the lineage from PPO’s reliance on auxiliary value networks to GRPO’s elegant elimination thereof via group-relative comparisons. Each step reveals trade-offs between bias reduction and implementation complexity, culminating in a critique that sets the stage for identifying fundamental flaws in token-centric importance weighting schemes prevalent in earlier iterations. As you read through this chapter, keep in mind that every equation presented represents a deliberate engineering choice made to balance computational feasibility with statistical consistency—a theme that recurs throughout modern AI research literature.

---

# Notation and Problem Formulation in Autoregressive Generation

To reason about reinforcement learning agents operating within language domains, we must first establish a standardized notation system rooted in probabilistic graphical models. Consider an autoregressive language model parametrized by $\theta$, which functions as our policy $\pi_\theta$. This policy maps input prompts or queries $x$ drawn from some dataset $\mathcal{D}$ to output sequences $y$, known as responses. The generation process is inherently sequential: starting from scratch or conditioned on initial prompt tokens $x$, the model predicts each successive token $y_t$ based solely on preceding history up to index $t-1$. Mathematically, this conditional independence assumption implies that the joint probability mass function over an entire response string decomposes into a product of marginal probabilities at each time step:

$$ \pi_\theta(y|x) = \prod_{t=1}^{|y|} \pi_\theta(y_t | x, y_{<t}) $$

Here, $|y|$ denotes the total number of tokens comprising response $y$, including potentially special termination symbols if applicable though strictly speaking treated simply as part of sequence # Chapter 3: Diagnosing Instability in Current Methods

## Introduction to Algorithmic Stability in Large-Scale Reinforcement Learning

As researchers strive to push the boundaries of large language models (LLMs) through reinforcement learning (RL), the ability to maintain stable training dynamics becomes paramount. While recent advancements have demonstrated that massive computational investments can unlock capabilities akin to solving competition-level mathematics or coding problems, the path to these breakthroughs is fraught with perilous instabilities. State-of-the-art algorithms designed specifically for LLMs, most notably Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), were expected to facilitate this scaling efficiently by removing the need for separate value models.然而，empirical observations reveal that when trained on gigantic architectures—particularly those involving high degrees of sparsity like Mixture-of-Experts (MoE)—these methods frequently succumb to "catastrophic model collapse." This phenomenon represents a complete degradation of model quality that renders subsequent training futile, regardless of hyperparameter tuning or architectural adjustments. Understanding why these failures occur requires dissecting the fundamental statistical assumptions underlying GRPO's design, revealing deep-seated conflicts between the algorithmic machinery used for small-scale problems and the realities of modern, ultra-large-scale generative systems.

The central thesis emerging from recent investigations is that the instability inherent in GRPO is not merely a matter of poor hyperparameter selection or insufficient computation; it arises from a structural flaw in how probability distributions are approximated during optimization. Specifically, the algorithm attempts to apply principles of importance sampling—a technique originally developed for estimating expectations over broad populations—to individual token predictions within a single sentence or code snippet. When scaled up to generate thousands of tokens or train billions of parameters, this local application breaks down statistically. The very mechanism meant to stabilize learning—the calculation of importance ratios—becomes a source of overwhelming noise. By tracing the lineage of this error back to its roots in probability theory and observing its destructive interaction with clipping mechanisms, we gain critical insight into why current pipelines fail and what must be replaced to achieve future scalability. This diagnosis forms the necessary groundwork for understanding proposed solutions like Group Sequence Policy Optimization (GSPO).

---

## The Principle of Importance Sampling: Behavior vs. Target Distributions

At the heart of many offline reinforcement learning techniques lies the concept of importance sampling, a rigorous method used to evaluate policies or estimate outcomes when direct access to the optimal policy is unavailable. Fundamentally, importance sampling addresses a scenario where one wishes to calculate the expected value of some function $f(z)$ under a *target* distribution $\pi_{\text{tar}}$, yet all available data comes exclusively from a *behavior* distribution $\pi_{\text{beh}}$. If one were naively averaged values from $\pi_{\text{beh}}$ without adjustment, the results would systematically bias toward regions favored by $\pi_{\text{beh}}$ rather than reflecting reality under $\pi_{\text{tar}}$. Corrective weighting is therefore required to bridge this gap. Mathematically, if $Z$ is a random variable representing a trajectory or outcome sampled from $\pi_{\text{beh}}$, then $Y'$ can be viewed as having been drawn from $\pi_{\text{tar}}$ via reweighting:
$$
\mathbb{E}_{z \sim \pi_{\text{tar}}} \left[ f(z) \right] = \mathbb{E}_{z \sim \pi_{\text{beh}}} \left[ \frac{\pi_{\text{tar}}(z)}{\pi_{\text{beh}}(z)} f(z) \right].
$$
Here, the ratio $\rho = \frac{\pi_{\text{tar}}(z)}{\pi_{\text{beh}}(z)}$ acts as an importance weight that compensates for discrepancies between how likely an event was under the two different distributions. Crucially, this equality holds strictly because it assumes we possess enough samples—or rely on averaging properties—that account for global differences between $\pi_{\text{tar}}$ and $\pi_{\text{beh}}$. The validity of this estimator depends heavily on reducing variance; ideally, we want samples drawn frequently enough that rare events under one distribution but common under another are adequately represented and corrected proportionately. Without sufficient sampling density relative to population heterogeneity, these corrections become unstable estimators prone to extreme outliers that distort gradients entirely away from useful directions.

When translating abstract importance sampling logic into concrete Deep Learning applications like Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) or GRPO, practitioners map $z$ onto sequences generated by language models and utilize ratios computed locally at every decision point along those trajectories. In PPO-style approaches applied sequentially word-by-word or character-by-character over text generation steps $t=1,\dots,T$, each step involves calculating an immediate transition-specific weight based solely upon conditional probabilities predicted immediately prior ($t-1$). Herein lies a subtle yet profound conceptual shift: whereas classical statistics might demand aggregate averages across vast swaths of possibilities before drawing conclusions about shifts in expectation densities... real-world implementations attempt instantaneous recalibrations conditioned purely upon single stochastic draws occurring moments ago during rollouts performed earlier ("cold" policies versus live ones now undergoing modifications).
